1	# Field Data Capture App — Architecture Analysis
2	
3	**Date:** 2026-05-22
4	**Context:** Building a field-facing app that captures inventory / SOP-task / maintenance-task data on-site (QR scan, photos, sensor checks), lets the operator decide whether to push the structured result to WhatsApp or to our own backend, and connects into the central Office Operations intelligence engine.
5	
6	**Target users:** Low-literacy operators (housekeeping, maintenance, stockroom staff). Low-end Android devices (≤2 GB RAM, Android 8–11, intermittent 3G/4G).
7	
8	---
9	
10	# first read the research on inventory Management [Research_on_Inventory_Management.md](./Research_on_Inventory_Management.md)
11	
12	## 1. Hard requirements vs. what each platform can actually deliver
13	
14	| Capability | PWA (browser) | TWA (PWA wrapped in Android shell) | React Native / Flutter (native) |
15	|---|---|---|---|
16	| Camera (photo) | ✅ `getUserMedia` / `<input capture>` | ✅ | ✅ full control (resolution, flash, focus) |
17	| QR / barcode scan | ⚠️ via `BarcodeDetector` — **missing on most low-end Androids and iOS Safari**; fallback is a JS lib like `zxing-wasm` which is ~1–3 MB and slow on cheap CPUs | Same as PWA | ✅ ML Kit native — fast, works offline, handles damaged codes |
18	| GPS / strict geofence | ✅ but the user can deny once and you can't re-prompt cleanly; no background location | ✅ slightly better | ✅ background + foreground, configurable accuracy, mock-location detection |
19	| Wi-Fi SSID / BSSID check | ❌ **not exposed to browsers** at all. You can only see "online/offline" | ❌ | ✅ can read SSID (with location permission), verify the operator is actually on the site's Wi-Fi |
20	| Bluetooth (beacons, BLE tags, printers) | ⚠️ Web Bluetooth — **Chrome Android only, not iOS, requires user gesture per device**, no background scan | ⚠️ same | ✅ full BLE stack, beacon ranging, background scan |
21	| Offline cache | ✅ Service Worker + IndexedDB | ✅ | ✅ SQLite / WatermelonDB / MMKV |
22	| Offline **queue with retries across reboots** | ⚠️ Background Sync API is Chromium-only and the OS can kill it | ⚠️ same | ✅ WorkManager (Android) guarantees delivery |
23	| Push notifications | ✅ Android / ❌ iOS Safari is flaky | ✅ Android | ✅ FCM, full reliability |
24	| App-icon install, no URL bar | ⚠️ "Add to home screen" — users get confused | ✅ looks like an app | ✅ |
25	| Install size | ~0 (loaded over network first time) | 3–5 MB shell + cache | 15–40 MB |
26	| First-load on 3G | 2–8 s painful | Same on first run, instant after | Instant (already installed) |
27	| Update path | Instant, no user action | Instant for web content | Play Store / sideload — users must accept |
28	| Locking the user into kiosk mode / single app | ❌ | ⚠️ partial | ✅ device-owner mode possible |
29	| Mock-GPS / rooted-device detection | ❌ | ❌ | ✅ |
30	
31	**Reading the table:** the deal-breakers for a pure PWA are **Wi-Fi SSID verification, reliable BLE, mock-location detection, and guaranteed offline queue delivery**. Each one is explicitly in your requirements.
32	
33	---
34	
35	## 2. Recommendation
36	
37	**Build a native Android app (one codebase via React Native or Flutter), distributed as an APK / private Play track. Treat iOS as out-of-scope for v1 — your user base is on cheap Android.**
38	
39	Reasons, in priority order:
40	
41	1. **Wi-Fi SSID check and BLE are non-negotiable** for your "is the operator actually on site" guard. Browsers cannot do this. Anything you build as a PWA will need a native companion anyway — at which point the PWA is dead weight.
42	2. **Offline queue with guaranteed delivery** matters more for low-literacy users than for power users. A power user will notice "my submission didn't sync" and retry; your users will not. WorkManager + a local SQLite outbox is the only reliable answer.
43	3. **QR scanning on a ₹6k Android phone** with `zxing-wasm` in a browser is genuinely bad — 1–3 seconds per scan, fails on glare or worn labels. ML Kit native scans in <200 ms and handles damaged codes. For a worker scanning 50 items a shift this is the difference between "works" and "abandoned."
44	4. **UI affordances for low-literacy users** — big tap targets, voice prompts, haptic feedback on success, locked orientation, no browser chrome stealing screen space, no accidental swipe-to-back closing the app. Native gives you all of this; PWAs leak browser behavior.
45	5. **Update discipline** — yes, the Play Store update path is slower than PWA, but you can use Play's in-app update API to force critical updates, and you avoid the class of bugs where a stale service worker serves a broken UI for days.
46	
47	### When a PWA *would* be the right call (not your case)
48	
49	- Users are office knowledge workers on modern phones.
50	- No SSID / BLE / background sync requirements.
51	- You want zero-install adoption.
52	- You're shipping a v0 prototype and want to test concept before committing.
53	
54	If you want a 2-week prototype to validate the workflow with one site before building the real thing, a PWA is fine for that throwaway. Do not let the prototype become the product.
55	
56	---
57	
58	## 3. Proposed system structure
59	
60	```
61	┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
62	│ Field Android App (RN or Flutter)                            │
63	│                                                              │
64	│  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐   │
65	│  │ Capture      │  │ Pre-flight   │  │ Routing decision │   │
66	│  │ • QR (ML Kit)│  │ • GPS fence  │  │ • To WhatsApp    │   │
67	│  │ • Photo      │  │ • SSID match │  │ • To backend     │   │
68	│  │ • Form       │  │ • BLE beacon │  │ • To both        │   │
69	│  │ • Voice note │  │ • Mock-loc?  │  │                  │   │
70	│  └──────┬───────┘  └──────┬───────┘  └────────┬─────────┘   │
71	│         │                 │                   │             │
72	│         ▼                 ▼                   ▼             │
73	│  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
74	│  │ Local SQLite outbox (WatermelonDB)                   │   │
75	│  │ • capture rows + photo blobs                         │   │
76	│  │ • status: draft / queued / sent / acked              │   │
77	│  └────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┘   │
78	│                           │                                 │
79	│                           ▼                                 │
80	│  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
81	│  │ WorkManager sync worker (exponential backoff)        │   │
82	│  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
83	└────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┘
84	                             │ HTTPS (multipart for photos)
85	                             ▼
86	┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
87	│ Edge ingest service (single endpoint)                       │
88	│   POST /v1/capture                                          │
89	│   • validates schema                                        │
90	│   • stores photo in object storage, returns CDN URL         │
91	│   • emits event → intelligence engine                       │
92	│   • fan-out:                                                │
93	│       ├─ if route=whatsapp → WhatsApp Cloud API template    │
94	│       ├─ if route=backend  → Office Ops core                │
95	│       └─ always → audit log + intelligence engine           │
96	└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
97	```
98	
99	### Key design choices
100	
101	**One ingest endpoint, server-side fan-out.** Do *not* let the phone call WhatsApp Cloud API directly. Reasons: WhatsApp tokens would leak onto devices, template approval is centralized, retries and rate-limits need a server. The phone tells the server "route this to WhatsApp" and the server does the WhatsApp call.
102	
103	**The "route" choice is metadata, not two code paths.** The app sends the same JSON regardless; a `destinations: ["whatsapp", "backend"]` field tells the server where to fan out. This keeps the app dumb and the policy on the server, where you can change it without re-releasing the APK.
104	
105	**Photos go to object storage, not embedded in JSON.** The app uploads the photo in the same multipart request, the server replaces it with a CDN URL before fan-out. WhatsApp templates support media; passing a URL is cheaper than re-uploading.
106	
107	**Pre-flight checks run before submit, and their results are signed into the payload.** GPS lat/long, SSID hash, beacon ID, timestamp, device ID — these become tamper-evident evidence on the server side. Don't trust them blindly (a determined user can patch the APK) but log them for audit.
108	
109	**Intelligence engine subscribes to the event stream, never polls the database.** The ingest service emits a `capture.created` event; the intelligence engine consumes it. This decouples the field app from intelligence-engine availability — if the engine is down, captures still land and get processed when it recovers.
110	
111	---
112	
113	## 4. UX considerations for low-literacy users
114	
115	These are platform-independent but easier to enforce in native:
116	
117	- **Icons + colors over text.** Green check, red cross, big camera icon. Where text is needed, use the local language and offer audio playback of the prompt.
118	- **Voice-prompt every screen.** Tap the screen title → it reads aloud. Cheap to add, transformative for adoption.
119	- **One action per screen.** Don't show a form with 8 fields. Scan QR → confirm item with photo of the label → enter quantity with +/- buttons → done.
120	- **Haptic + sound on success.** Workers stop trusting silent confirmations.
121	- **No "submit" word.** Use a big green checkmark button. Use "send" only after the action is irreversible.
122	- **Show queue state plainly.** A badge "3 waiting to send" on the home screen, not buried in settings. When it clears to 0, celebrate it visibly.
123	- **Kiosk / single-app lock option** for site-owned devices, so workers can't accidentally exit to a browser or get stuck in a notification.
124	- **Pre-fill from context.** If the operator scans a QR on a fire extinguisher, the app already knows it's a maintenance task and skips the "what kind of task" question.
125	
126	---
127	
128	## 5. Tech stack suggestion
129	
130	| Layer | Choice | Why |
131	|---|---|---|
132	| App framework | **React Native** (or Flutter if the team prefers Dart) | Native modules for ML Kit, BLE, Wi-Fi; one codebase; large hiring pool |
133	| Local DB | **WatermelonDB** on SQLite | Reactive, scales to 10k+ rows on cheap phones, plays well with sync |
134	| Sync | **WorkManager** via a native module | Survives reboot, OS-managed backoff |
135	| QR/Barcode | **ML Kit** (`@react-native-ml-kit/barcode-scanning`) | Free, on-device, fast |
136	| Photos | `react-native-vision-camera` | Lower-level control than `expo-camera`; needed for compression tuning on cheap devices |
137	| Geofence | `react-native-geolocation-service` + server-side validation | Don't trust the phone alone |
138	| BLE | `react-native-ble-plx` | Mature, beacon support |
139	| Wi-Fi SSID | `react-native-wifi-reborn` | Reads SSID with location permission |
140	| Push | FCM via `@react-native-firebase/messaging` | Reliable, free |
141	| Auth | OIDC + short-lived JWT, refresh on Wi-Fi | Don't ship long-lived secrets to the device |
142	| Distribution | **Private Play track** (preferred) or signed APK + MDM | Auto-updates, rollback |
143	| Crash / telemetry | Sentry + a custom event for "capture sent" success rate | The metric that matters |
144	
145	---
146	
147	## 6. What I would **not** do
148	
149	- **Don't build a PWA first "to learn" and plan to rewrite.** You will not rewrite. The PWA will accrete features and the day you need SSID checks you'll be stuck.
150	- **Don't let the phone hit WhatsApp Cloud API directly.** Token sprawl, no rate-limiting, no audit.
151	- **Don't write your own QR decoder.** ML Kit is free and better than anything you'll ship in a year.
152	- **Don't make the operator choose "WhatsApp or backend" on every submit.** Choose a sensible default per task type on the server, let them override only when needed.
153	- **Don't gate submission on connectivity.** Capture first, sync later. Always.
154	- **Don't try to make iOS work in v1** unless a real customer asks. Every cross-platform compromise is a tax on the 95% case.
155	
156	---
157	
158	## 7. Phased rollout
159	
160	**Phase 1 (4–6 weeks):** Android app with QR scan + photo + form + offline outbox + one ingest endpoint + WhatsApp template fan-out. No BLE, no SSID check yet. Pilot at one site.
161	
162	**Phase 2 (3–4 weeks):** Add GPS geofence, SSID check, signed pre-flight payloads, audit dashboard. Connect to intelligence engine event stream.
163	
164	**Phase 3 (4 weeks):** BLE beacons for indoor location, voice prompts, kiosk mode for site-owned devices, in-app updates.
165	
166	**Phase 4:** iOS if and only if customer demand justifies it.
167	
168	---
169	
170	## 8. Open questions to resolve before coding
171	
172	1. **Who owns the device?** Site-owned (kiosk mode possible, you can pre-provision) vs. operator-owned (BYOD, harder UX, training cost). Changes the answer for distribution and security.
173	2. **WhatsApp account model.** One business number per site, or one central number with site identifier in the template? Affects template approval count and reply routing.
174	3. **What happens to a capture sent to WhatsApp — does anyone *act* on it, or is it informational?** If actionable, the WhatsApp reply needs to flow back into the intelligence engine, which means you need WhatsApp webhook handling on the server.
175	4. **Photo retention policy.** Cheap phones fill up fast. Local cache must auto-evict after server-ack.
176	5. **Language coverage.** Hindi + English minimum? Regional languages? Voice prompts must be pre-recorded per language.
177	6. **What's the failure UX when a pre-flight check fails?** ("You are not on site Wi-Fi" — can they override with a supervisor PIN? Or hard block?)
178	
179	Answer these six before locking the spec.
```

1	# PWA Prototype vs React Native: Decision for Inventory Field App
2	
3	## Your Specific Workflows (Mapped Out)
4	
5	Before deciding the platform, let's be precise about what you're actually building:
6	
7	```mermaid
8	flowchart TD
9	    A["📋 DMart Bill / Receipt\n(photo via Slack / Telegram / Dashboard)"] -->|"Server-side AI extraction"| B["Extract line items:\nproduct, qty, price"]
10	    B --> C["Update Orders List\n(expected deliveries)"]
11	  
12	    C -.->|"Synced to field app"| D["🚪 Gate Entry\n(Guard scans items arriving)"]
13	    D --> E{"Barcode known?"}
14	  
15	    E -->|Yes| F["Auto-identify product\n+ show expected qty"]
16	    E -->|No| G["📝 Map barcode → product\n(one-time registration)"]
17	    G --> F
18	  
19	    F --> H{"Qty matches order?"}
20	    H -->|"✅ Match"| I["Mark as received\nUpdate inventory"]
21	    H -->|"❌ Mismatch"| J["Flag discrepancy\nAlert admin"]
22	  
23	    I --> K["🔮 Future: Proof Photo\n(AI verification)"]
24	    J --> K
25	```
26	
27	> [!NOTE]
28	> **Bill photos are NOT captured from the field app.** They arrive through existing channels (Slack, Telegram, admin dashboard) and are processed server-side by the intelligence engine. The field app's only job is **gate scanning + product mapping**.
29	
30	### The 5 Core Capabilities You Need
31	
32	| #   | Capability                               | Where it runs                                               | What it requires                                                       | PWA can do it?                                      | Native can do it?                         |
33	| --- | ---------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------- |
34	| 1   | **Extract bill data from DMart receipt** | **Server-side** (bill arrives via Slack/Telegram/Dashboard) | AI OCR extraction pipeline                                             | N/A — not a field app concern                       | N/A — not a field app concern             |
35	| 2   | **Barcode scanning at gate**             | **Field app**                                               | Fast, reliable barcode reader. Guard scanning 20-50 items per delivery | ⚠️**Slow on cheap phones** (~1-3s/scan with JS libs) | ✅ ML Kit: <200ms, handles glare           |
36	| 3   | **Barcode ↔ product mapping**            | **Field app**                                               | Database lookup + registration form                                    | ✅ Works fine                                        | ✅ Works fine                              |
37	| 4   | **Quantity validation**                  | **Field app** (against server-synced orders)                | Compare scanned count vs order                                         | ✅ Works fine                                        | ✅ Works fine                              |
38	| 5   | **Proof photo (future)**                 | **Field app**                                               | High-quality photo with metadata, tamper-evidence                      | ⚠️ Limited camera control                            | ✅ Full control: resolution, EXIF, signing |
39	
40	---
41	
42	## My Recommendation: **Skip the PWA. Build React Native directly.**
43	
44	Here's why this is different from the usual "prototype first" advice:
45	
46	### Reason 1: Barcode scanning is the central interaction — and it's the PWA's biggest weakness
47	
48	Your guard is standing at a gate, scanning items as they come off a truck. This is **not** a "scan one QR occasionally" use case. This is **rapid sequential scanning** — 20-50 items per delivery, potentially multiple deliveries per day.
49	
50	On a ₹6-8K Android phone (which is what your guard has):
51	
52	- **PWA with zxing-wasm**: 1-3 seconds per scan, fails on damaged/worn barcodes, struggles with glare
53	- **Native ML Kit**: <200ms per scan, handles damaged codes, works in poor lighting
54	
55	At 30 items per delivery, that's the difference between **1 minute** (native) and **1.5 minutes with frustrating failures** (PWA). The guard will abandon the app after day 2.
56	
57	### Reason 2: The "throwaway prototype" never gets thrown away
58	
59	Your own architecture doc says it best:
60	
61	> Don't build a PWA first "to learn" and plan to rewrite. You will not rewrite. The PWA will accrete features and the day you need SSID checks you'll be stuck.
62	
63	This is especially true for you because the barcode-product mapping database you build during the PWA phase **is the valuable data**. Once you have 200 products mapped, migrating that AND retraining staff on a new app is more expensive than building native from day 1.
64	
65	### Reason 3: The workflows you described are the MVP — they're not complex enough to need "concept validation"
66	
67	Let me be blunt: the concept is already validated. You know:
68	
69	- Guards need to scan incoming items ✅
70	- Bills need to be digitized ✅
71	- Barcodes need to map to products ✅
72	- Quantities need validation ✅
73	
74	There's no product-market-fit risk here. This is an internal operations tool for a known workflow. You don't need a PWA to "test if people will use it" — they'll use it because it's their job and you'll mandate it.
75	
76	### Reason 4: The offline queue matters for gate entry
77	
78	When a truck arrives at 7 AM and the internet is spotty, the guard **must** be able to scan everything and sync later. Your architecture doc correctly identifies that:
79	
80	- PWA `Background Sync` is Chromium-only and the OS can kill it
81	- Native `WorkManager` guarantees delivery across reboots
82	
83	A half-synced delivery is worse than a paper log.
84	
85	---
86	
87	## What Would Change My Mind (When a PWA IS Right)
88	
89	A PWA would make sense if:
90	
91	- ❌ Your primary users were office staff on modern phones (not guards on cheap Androids)
92	- ❌ Scanning was occasional, not the core workflow
93	- ❌ You had genuine uncertainty about whether the workflow would work at all
94	- ❌ You needed zero-install adoption across many untrained users
95	
96	None of these apply to your case.
97	
98	---
99	
100	## Recommended Approach: React Native, Phased Build
101	
102	### Phase 1 — Field App: Core Scanner + Mapping (3-4 weeks)
103	
104	Build the minimum that makes gate entry digital:
105	
106	| Feature              | Details                                                                       |
107	| -------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
108	| **Barcode scanner**  | ML Kit via `@react-native-ml-kit/barcode-scanning`                            |
109	| **Product registry** | Scan barcode → if unknown, register (name, category, unit) → save to local DB |
110	| **Gate entry flow**  | Guard scans items → app shows product info → guard confirms quantity          |
111	| **Offline outbox**   | WatermelonDB + WorkManager. Everything works without internet.                |
112	| **Order list sync**  | Orders created server-side (from bill extraction) sync down to the field app  |
113	
114	> [!IMPORTANT]
115	> Start with the **barcode → product mapping** flow. This is your data flywheel. Every scan enriches your product database. Within 2-3 weeks of real use, most common items will be registered.
116	
117	### Phase 2 — Server-side: Bill Extraction Pipeline (2-3 weeks, parallel track)
118	
119	This runs **entirely on the backend** — no field app changes needed:
120	
121	| Feature                      | Where     | Details                                                                          |
122	| ---------------------------- | --------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
123	| **Bill photo ingestion**     | Server    | Receive bill photos from Slack, Telegram, or admin dashboard                     |
124	| **AI bill extraction**       | Server    | Photo → Claude/Gemini OCR → structured line items (product, qty, price)          |
125	| **Order creation**           | Server    | Extracted bill data auto-creates "expected delivery" entries                     |
126	| **Quantity mismatch alerts** | Server    | Compare gate scan totals (from field app) vs bill line items, flag discrepancies |
127	| **Admin review UI**          | Dashboard | Admin reviews/corrects AI-extracted data before confirming order                 |
128	
129	> [!TIP]
130	> Phase 1 and Phase 2 can run **in parallel**. The field app works standalone with manually-entered orders while the bill extraction pipeline is being built. Once Phase 2 is live, orders auto-populate from bills.
131	
132	### Phase 3 — Intelligence + Proof (3-4 weeks, future)
133	
134	| Feature                 | Where                   | Details                                                                       |
135	| ----------------------- | ----------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
136	| **Proof photos**        | Field app               | Photo of received goods, timestamped, GPS-tagged, stored with delivery record |
137	| **AI verification**     | Server                  | Compare proof photo contents against expected items                           |
138	| **Analytics dashboard** | Dashboard               | Delivery accuracy rates, vendor reliability scores, consumption patterns      |
139	| **Reorder alerts**      | Server → Slack/Telegram | Trigger when stock drops below threshold                                      |
140	
141	---
142	
143	## The Barcode Mapping Flow (Your Most Important Data Asset)
144	
145	This deserves special attention because it's the foundation of everything:
146	
147	```
148	┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
149	│ FIRST SCAN (barcode unknown)                            │
150	│                                                          │
151	│  Guard scans: 8901234567890                              │
152	│       ↓                                                  │
153	│  App: "New product! What is this?"                       │
154	│       ↓                                                  │
155	│  Guard sees: [📷 Photo of product]                       │
156	│              [Name: ____________]   ← big tap keyboard   │
157	│              [Category: ▼ Grocery / Cleaning / Office]   │
158	│              [Unit: ▼ Piece / Kg / Litre / Pack]         │
159	│       ↓                                                  │
160	│  Saved: barcode 8901234567890 = "Tata Salt 1kg"          │
161	│                                                          │
162	├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
163	│ EVERY SUBSEQUENT SCAN                                    │
164	│                                                          │
165	│  Guard scans: 8901234567890                              │
166	│       ↓                                                  │
167	│  App instantly shows:                                    │
168	│  ┌──────────────────────────────────┐                    │
169	│  │  🧂 Tata Salt 1kg               │                    │
170	│  │  Expected: 10 units (from order) │                    │
171	│  │  Scanned so far: 7              │                    │
172	│  │                                  │                    │
173	│  │  [+1] [+5] [Custom qty]          │                    │
174	│  └──────────────────────────────────┘                    │
175	│                                                          │
176	└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
177	```
178	
179	> [!TIP]
180	> **Accelerator idea**: For the first batch of products, instead of making the guard register each one, extract the product list from DMart bills first. The AI extracts `{name, qty, price}` from the receipt — you pre-populate the product registry. When the guard scans at the gate, most items are already known.
181	
182	---
183	
184	## Open Questions for You
185	
186	1. **Who is the guard?** Is it a dedicated security person, or a rotating staff member? This affects training investment and whether you can use device-owner/kiosk mode.
187	2. **How many unique products do you typically receive?** If it's 50-100 recurring items, the barcode mapping database fills up in 1-2 weeks. If it's 500+, you may want to consider pre-loading from a public barcode database (like Open Food Facts for grocery items).
188	3. **DMart bills — are they always printed (thermal receipt), or sometimes handwritten?** This affects whether the server-side AI extraction needs general OCR or can use structured receipt parsing.
189	4. **Which channels will bill photos come from most often?** Slack, Telegram, admin dashboard upload — or all three? This determines how many ingestion integrations the backend pipeline needs.
190	5. **Should the AI auto-create orders from bills, or should an admin review/approve first?** Auto-create is faster but risks bad OCR creating wrong orders. Admin review adds a human checkpoint but adds latency.
191	6. **Is quantity validation per-item (scan each individual item) or per-line-item (scan one, enter count)?** Per-item is more accurate but slower; per-line-item with manual count entry is faster but relies on the guard counting correctly.
192	
193	---
194	
195	## Bottom Line
196	
197	| Path                      | Verdict                                                                                                                                                          |
198	| ------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
199	| **PWA prototype first**   | ❌ Don't do this. Your core workflow (rapid barcode scanning) is the exact thing PWAs are worst at. You'll build it, hate the scanning, then build native anyway. |
200	| **React Native directly** | ✅**Do this.** The workflows are well-understood, the use case is internal (no product-market-fit risk), and the barcode scanning quality gap is a dealbreaker.   |
201	
202	Start with Phase 1 (scanner + mapping). You can have a guard scanning items at the gate within 3-4 weeks. The bill extraction and AI features layer on top of a working foundation.
```

```
1	the barcodes we have behind the product like packed groceries, what are the used for? 
2	
3	The barcodes behind products like groceries are mainly used for identification, tracking, billing, and inventory management across the entire supply chain.
4	
5	The most common one you see is called a UPC or EAN barcode.
6	
7	Here is what happens behind the scenes:
8	
9	1. Product Identification
10	
11	Each barcode represents a unique product ID.
12	
13	For example:
14	
15	A packet of Oreo biscuits 100g might have:
16	8901234567890
17	
18	That number identifies:
19	
20	1. Brand
21	2. Product type
22	3. Variant
23	4. Size
24	5. Manufacturer
25	
26	So when scanned, the system instantly knows:
27	“this is Oreo vanilla cream 100g”
28	
29	Not just “biscuits”.
30	
31	Think of it like a digital fingerprint for products.
32	
33	⸻
34	
35	2. Billing at Checkout
36	
37	At supermarkets, the cashier scans the barcode using a laser or camera scanner.
38	
39	The POS system then:
40	
41	1. Finds the product in the database
42	2. Fetches:
43	    1. Name
44	    2. Price
45	    3. Tax
46	    4. Discounts
47	3. Adds it to the bill instantly
48	
49	Without barcode systems:
50	
51	1. Staff would manually type product names
52	2. Billing would be slower
53	3. Mistakes would increase
54	
55	⸻
56	
57	3. Inventory Management
58	
59	This is one of the biggest uses.
60	
61	Every scan updates stock count automatically.
62	
63	Example flow:
64	
65	1. Warehouse receives 500 Maggi packets
66	2. System records inventory
67	3. Products go to shelves
68	4. Customers buy 120 packets
69	5. Barcode scans reduce stock count automatically
70	6. System alerts when stock becomes low
71	
72	Large companies heavily depend on this.
73	
74	This is how:
75	
76	1. Amazon
77	2. Dmart
78	3. Reliance Fresh
79	4. Walmart
80	
81	track millions of products accurately.
82	
83	⸻
84	
85	4. Supply Chain Tracking
86	
87	Barcodes help track products across:
88	
89	1. Factory
90	2. Warehouse
91	3. Trucks
92	4. Stores
93	5. Customers
94	
95	This enables:
96	
97	1. Shipment tracking
98	2. Batch tracking
99	3. Recall management
100	4. Expiry management
101	
102	Example:
103	If a milk batch becomes contaminated, the company can identify exactly which stores received that batch.
104	
105	⸻
106	
107	5. Faster Warehousing
108	
109	Workers use handheld barcode scanners.
110	
111	They scan:
112	
113	1. Incoming products
114	2. Shelf locations
115	3. Shipment boxes
116	
117	This helps:
118	
119	1. Reduce human error
120	2. Increase speed
121	3. Automate logistics
122	
123	Modern warehouses combine:
124	
125	1. Barcodes
126	2. RFID
127	3. AI systems
128	4. Robotics
129	
130	⸻
131	
132	6. Product Information Lookup
133	
134	Sometimes the barcode itself only stores an ID.
135	
136	The actual information is stored in a database.
137	
138	So the flow is:
139	
140	Barcode → Product ID → Database lookup → Full product data
141	
142	Exactly like:
143	User ID → Database → User profile
144	
145	As a developer, you can think of barcode as a primary key.
146	
147	⸻
148	
149	Types of Product Codes
150	
151	UPC
152	
153	Mostly used in the US.
154	
155	EAN
156	
157	International standard.
158	Very common in India.
159	
160	QR Code
161	
162	Stores much more data directly.
163	Can contain:
164	
165	1. URLs
166	2. Payment data
167	3. Contact info
168	4. Authentication tokens
169	
170	RFID Tags
171	
172	Advanced version using radio waves.
173	No direct line of sight needed.
174	
175	You were already exploring this world with your RC522 and NFC setup. NFC is actually a specialized subset of RFID.
176	
177	⸻
178	
179	How Barcode Scanners Work
180	
181	Older scanners:
182	
183	1. Emit laser light
184	2. Read reflected patterns
185	
186	Black bars absorb light.
187	White spaces reflect light.
188	
189	The scanner converts this into binary data.
190	
191	Modern scanners:
192	
193	1. Use cameras
194	2. Decode via image processing
195	
196	Phones can do this too.
197	
198	⸻
199	
200	Interesting Engineering Insight
201	
202	A barcode itself usually does NOT contain:
203	
204	1. Price
205	2. Product name
206	3. Inventory count
207	
208	It mainly contains an identifier.
209	
210	The intelligence comes from:
211	
212	1. Databases
213	2. POS software
214	3. Inventory systems
215	4. ERP systems
216	
217	The barcode is just the bridge between physical and digital worlds.
218	
219	That simple set of black lines is actually part of a massive global synchronization system connecting:
220	
221	1. Manufacturers
222	2. Warehouses
223	3. Retailers
224	4. Logistics
225	5. Finance systems
226	6. Consumers
227	
228	It is one of the most successful low cost engineering standards ever created.
229	
230	---
231	
232	like how does the barcode is created, like I just see numbers when I scan the barcode, are they just UID or combination of information?
233	
234	Yes. In most grocery products, the barcode is mainly a structured unique identifier, not the full product information itself.
235	
236	The numbers are not random. They follow global standards.
237	
238	For example, a typical Indian grocery barcode may look like:
239	
240	8901234567890
241	
242	This is usually an EAN 13 barcode.
243	
244	Let’s break it down.
245	
246	Structure of a Barcode Number
247	
248	Example: EAN 13
249	
250	Format:
251	
252	XXX XXXXX XXXXX C
253	
254	Where:
255	
256	1. Prefix / Country code
257	2. Company identifier
258	3. Product identifier
259	4. Check digit
260	
261	⸻
262	
263	1. Country / GS1 Prefix
264	
265	Example:
266	890
267	
268	This indicates the barcode was registered in India.
269	
270	Some common prefixes:
271	
272	1. 890 → India
273	2. 000 to 139 → USA/Canada
274	3. 690 to 699 → China
275	
276	Important:
277	This does NOT always mean the product was manufactured there.
278	It mainly means the barcode was registered there.
279	
280	⸻
281	
282	2. Company Identifier
283	
284	Example:
285	12345
286	
287	Assigned by an organization called GS1.
288	
289	This identifies the company.
290	
291	Think of it like:
292	Company namespace.
293	
294	⸻
295	
296	3. Product Identifier
297	
298	Example:
299	67890
300	
301	Chosen by the company itself.
302	
303	This differentiates:
304	
305	1. Flavors
306	2. Sizes
307	3. Variants
308	4. Packaging
309	
310	For example:
311	
312	1. Coke 250ml
313	2. Coke 500ml
314	3. Diet Coke
315	
316	All get different product IDs.
317	
318	⸻
319	
320	4. Check Digit
321	
322	Final digit:
323	0
324	
325	This is used for error detection.
326	
327	The scanner mathematically verifies:
328	“Was this barcode scanned correctly?”
329	
330	If the computed check digit mismatches:
331	
332	1. Scan fails
333	2. System retries
334	
335	This prevents many reading errors.
336	
337	⸻
338	
339	So What Does the Barcode Actually Store?
340	
341	Usually:
342	Just the numeric identifier.
343	
344	Example:
345	8901234567890
346	
347	The POS system then does:
348	
349	Scan barcode
350	   ↓
351	Get number
352	   ↓
353	Search database
354	   ↓
355	Retrieve:
356	    Name
357	    Price
358	    Tax
359	    Inventory
360	    Expiry
361	
362	So the barcode is more like:
363	A database key.
364	
365	Very similar to:
366	
367	SELECT * FROM products WHERE barcode='8901234567890';
368	
369	⸻
370	
371	How Are Black Lines Generated?
372	
373	This is where it gets interesting.
374	
375	The numbers are converted into patterns of:
376	
377	1. Black bars
378	2. White spaces
379	
380	Each digit has a binary-like visual encoding.
381	
382	Example idea:
383	
384	Digit 7 may become something like:
385	
386	1010001
387	
388	Which becomes:
389	
390	Black White Black White White White Black
391	
392	The scanner reads widths and spacing.
393	
394	So the barcode is essentially:
395	Visual binary encoding.
396	
397	⸻
398	
399	Important Insight
400	
401	The bars themselves are the real data.
402	
403	The numbers printed below are mainly for humans.
404	
405	Even if the printed numbers disappear:
406	A scanner can still read the bars.
407	
408	⸻
409	
410	Who Creates Barcodes?
411	
412	Companies register with GS1.
413	
414	Then they receive:
415	
416	1. Company prefix
417	2. Barcode ranges
418	
419	Then they generate product barcodes.
420	
421	⸻
422	
423	Can You Create Your Own Barcode?
424	
425	Technically yes.
426	
427	You can generate barcode images easily using libraries.
428	
429	Example in JavaScript:
430	
431	JsBarcode("#barcode", "8901234567890");
432	
433	Or Python:
434	
435	from barcode import EAN13
436	
437	But unofficial barcodes will not work properly in large retail ecosystems unless registered.
438	
439	⸻
440	
441	Advanced Types
442	
443	QR Codes
444	
445	Unlike normal barcodes:
446	QR codes can directly store:
447	
448	1. URLs
449	2. Text
450	3. JSON
451	4. Payment info
452	
453	A normal barcode is mostly:
454	Identifier only.
455	
456	A QR code is more like:
457	Portable storage.
458	
459	⸻
460	
461	RFID vs Barcode
462	
463	You were exploring NFC/RFID already, so here is the important distinction:
464	
465	Feature	Barcode	RFID/NFC
466	Needs line of sight	Yes	No
467	Stores data	Small	More
468	Read distance	Very short	Medium
469	Cost	Very cheap	More expensive
470	Scan multiple at once	No	Yes
471	
472	That is why grocery stores mostly use barcodes:
473	Extremely cheap and scalable.
474	
475	A printed barcode costs almost nothing.
476	
477	---
478	
479	so barcodes of a product change by batch? 
480	
481	Usually, no.
482	
483	The barcode of a product generally stays the same across batches.
484	
485	Example:
486	
487	Every 500g packet of a specific biscuit variant may use the same barcode for years.
488	
489	What changes between batches is usually:
490	
491	1. Manufacturing date
492	2. Expiry date
493	3. Batch/Lot number
494	
495	Those are often printed separately from the main barcode.
496	
497	So the flow is usually:
498	
499	Thing	Changes per batch?
500	Product barcode	No
501	Batch/Lot number	Yes
502	Manufacturing date	Yes
503	Expiry date	Yes
504	
505	⸻
506	
507	Why Keep Barcode Same?
508	
509	Because the barcode identifies the product type, not the individual item.
510	
511	Example:
512	
513	All these may share the same barcode:
514	
515	1. Maggi Masala 70g packet produced in January
516	2. Same product produced in February
517	3. Same product produced in March
518	
519	Because for billing and inventory:
520	Store only needs:
521	“this is Maggi 70g”
522	
523	Not:
524	“this specific packet from factory line 3 at 11:42 AM”
525	
526	⸻
527	
528	Then How Are Batches Tracked?
529	
530	Using:
531	
532	1. Batch number
533	2. Lot number
534	3. Sometimes secondary barcodes or QR codes
535	
536	Example:
537	
538	Barcode: 8901234567890
539	Batch: B240526A
540	MFG: 25/05/2026
541	EXP: 25/11/2026
542	
543	⸻
544	
545	Why Batch Numbers Matter
546	
547	They are critical for:
548	
549	1. Product Recall
550	
551	Suppose contamination happens in:
552	Batch B240526A
553	
554	Company can recall only affected packets instead of all products.
555	
556	Very important in:
557	
558	1. Food
559	2. Medicine
560	3. Cosmetics
561	
562	⸻
563	
564	2. Expiry Tracking
565	
566	Warehouses track:
567	
568	1. Which batch arrived first
569	2. Which expires sooner
570	
571	This enables:
572	FIFO or FEFO inventory systems.
573	
574	FIFO:
575	First In First Out
576	
577	FEFO:
578	First Expiry First Out
579	
580	⸻
581	
582	3. Quality Control
583	
584	If customers complain:
585	“this batch tastes weird”
586	
587	The company can:
588	
589	1. Identify production line
590	2. Check raw materials
591	3. Trace factory conditions
592	
593	⸻
594	
595	Do Some Systems Use Unique Codes Per Item?
596	
597	Yes.
598	
599	Especially in:
600	
601	1. Luxury products
602	2. Electronics
603	3. Pharmaceuticals
604	4. High value logistics
605	
606	They may use:
607	
608	1. QR codes
609	2. Serialized barcodes
610	3. RFID tags
611	
612	Where every single item gets a unique identity.
613	
614	Example:
615	iPhones have unique serial numbers.
616	
617	⸻
618	
619	In Pharmacies and Advanced Retail
620	
621	You may see:
622	
623	Main Barcode
624	
625	Identifies product type.
626	
627	Secondary Code
628	
629	Contains:
630	
631	1. Batch
632	2. Expiry
633	3. Serial number
634	
635	Often using:
636	
637	1. QR
638	2. DataMatrix
639	3. GS1 2D codes
640	
641	⸻
642	
643	Interesting Technical Insight
644	
645	Traditional barcodes are optimized for:
646	
647	1. Speed
648	2. Cheap printing
649	3. Universal compatibility
650	
651	That is why they intentionally keep data minimal.
652	
653	The real intelligence is stored in:
654	
655	1. ERP systems
656	2. Warehouse databases
657	3. POS systems
658	4. Supply chain software
659	
660	Modern systems are increasingly shifting toward:
661	
662	1. QR based traceability
663	2. RFID inventory
664	3. Real time digital twins of inventory
665	
666	Especially after e commerce and global logistics became massive.
667	
668	---
669	
670	how barcode are registered in mallsstores  and warehouse, like someone has to manually map the product info with barcode number?
671	
672	Yes. Somewhere in the system, product information must be mapped to the barcode number. But in modern retail, much of this is standardized and automated.
673	
674	The process is different for:
675	
676	1. Small shops
677	2. Supermarkets
678	3. Large retail chains
679	4. Warehouses
680	5. Manufacturers
681	
682	High Level Flow
683	
684	Manufacturer creates barcode
685	        ↓
686	Product enters distributor database
687	        ↓
688	Retailer imports product catalog
689	        ↓
690	POS/ERP maps barcode → product info
691	        ↓
692	Scanner reads barcode
693	        ↓
694	System fetches product details
695	
696	⸻
697	
698	Scenario 1: Small Shop
699	
700	In a local shop, yes, someone may manually map products.
701	
702	Example:
703	
704	Barcode: 8901234567890
705	Name: Maggi 70g
706	Price: ₹14
707	Stock: 120
708	
709	The shopkeeper may:
710	
711	1. Scan product once
712	2. Enter details manually
713	3. Save into POS software
714	
715	Next time:
716	Scanner automatically recognizes it.
717	
718	This is common in:
719	
720	1. Kirana stores
721	2. Small pharmacies
722	3. Small boutiques
723	
724	⸻
725	
726	Scenario 2: Supermarkets & Malls
727	
728	Large stores usually do NOT manually enter every product one by one.
729	
730	Instead they receive:
731	
732	1. Supplier catalogs
733	2. CSV files
734	3. ERP integrations
735	4. Vendor databases
736	
737	Example:
738	
739	A distributor sends:
740	
741	barcode,name,price,category
742	8901234567890,Maggi 70g,14,Noodles
743	8901234567891,Coke 500ml,40,Beverage
744	
745	The mall imports this into:
746	
747	1. POS
748	2. ERP
749	3. Inventory system
750	
751	Now all billing counters recognize products instantly.
752	
753	⸻
754	
755	Scenario 3: Warehouses
756	
757	Warehouses care more about:
758	
759	1. Storage location
760	2. Quantity
761	3. Movement
762	4. Batch tracking
763	
764	Their systems map:
765	
766	Barcode → SKU → Warehouse location
767	
768	Example:
769	
770	8901234567890
771	   ↓
772	SKU: MAGGI70
773	   ↓
774	Rack A3 Shelf 5
775	
776	Workers scan:
777	
778	1. Product barcode
779	2. Shelf barcode
780	
781	System updates inventory location.
782	
783	⸻
784	
785	What Is SKU?
786	
787	SKU = Stock Keeping Unit
788	
789	Internal company identifier.
790	
791	Important distinction:
792	
793	Thing	Purpose
794	Barcode	Universal product identity
795	SKU	Internal business identity
796	
797	Example:
798	
799	Barcode: 8901234567890
800	SKU: MAG-NDL-70-MAS
801	
802	Companies often use their own SKU system internally.
803	
804	⸻
805	
806	How Stores Initially Get Product Data
807	
808	Method 1: Manual Entry
809	
810	Small scale.
811	
812	Method 2: Supplier Feed
813	
814	Distributor sends product catalog.
815	
816	Method 3: ERP Integration
817	
818	Large chains integrate directly with suppliers.
819	
820	Method 4: Centralized Retail Database
821	
822	Corporate HQ manages all products centrally.
823	
824	Example:
825	Walmart stores don’t manually add products individually at every branch.
826	
827	Corporate systems distribute product master data across all stores.
828	
829	⸻
830	
831	What Happens During Billing?
832	
833	When scanner reads:
834	
835	8901234567890
836	
837	POS does:
838	
839	SELECT * FROM inventory
840	WHERE barcode='8901234567890';
841	
842	Returns:
843	
844	{
845	  "name": "Maggi 70g",
846	  "price": 14,
847	  "tax": "5%",
848	  "stock": 120
849	}
850	
851	Then:
852	
853	1. Bill updates
854	2. Stock reduces
855	3. Sales analytics update
856	
857	All in milliseconds.
858	
859	⸻
860	
861	In Large Warehouses
862	
863	Modern warehouses may use:
864	
865	1. Barcode scanners
866	2. RFID gates
867	3. Conveyor scanners
868	4. AI cameras
869	5. Autonomous robots
870	
871	Systems commonly involved:
872	
873	1. WMS
874	    Warehouse Management System
875	2. ERP
876	    Enterprise Resource Planning
877	3. OMS
878	    Order Management System
879	4. POS
880	    Point of Sale
881	
882	⸻
883	
884	Interesting Real World Insight
885	
886	A huge engineering challenge is not barcode scanning.
887	
888	It is maintaining accurate product master data.
889	
890	Problems include:
891	
892	1. Duplicate products
893	2. Wrong pricing
894	3. Mismatched barcodes
895	4. Supplier inconsistencies
896	5. Unit conversion issues
897	
898	Example:
899	One supplier writes:
900	“Coca Cola 500ML”
901	
902	Another:
903	“Coke 0.5L”
904	
905	Another:
906	“CocaCola Half Liter”
907	
908	Large companies spend massive effort normalizing this data.
909	
910	⸻
911	
912	This Is Why GS1 Exists
913	
914	GS1 helps standardize:
915	
916	1. Product identifiers
917	2. Company prefixes
918	3. Logistics codes
919	4. Supply chain interoperability
920	
921	Without standards:
922	Global retail would become chaos.
923	
924	⸻
925	
926	As a Developer
927	
928	You can think of the whole retail system as:
929	
930	Physical product
931	      ↕
932	Barcode
933	      ↕
934	Database record
935	      ↕
936	Business logic
937	
938	The barcode is simply the bridge connecting real world objects to software systems.
939	
940	That simple scan triggers:
941	
942	1. Inventory update
943	2. Financial transaction
944	3. Analytics event
945	4. Supply chain update
946	5. Tax calculation
947	6. Demand forecasting
948	7. Reordering logic
949	
950	All from a few black lines printed on plastic.
951	
952	---
953	
954	what tools for inventory management are present in the market today, and what would be the cost if I use them to maintain our office cafeteria? 
955	
956	For an office cafeteria, you do NOT need enterprise warehouse software initially.
957	
958	Your requirements are probably:
959	
960	1. Ingredient inventory tracking
961	2. Grocery stock management
962	3. Daily consumption tracking
963	4. Billing or employee meal tracking
964	5. Low stock alerts
965	6. Expiry monitoring
966	7. Vendor purchase tracking
967	8. Basic analytics
968	
969	That is a small to medium scale inventory + cafeteria management problem.
970	
971	⸻
972	
973	What Tools Exist Today?
974	
975	These are the most common categories:
976	
977	Type	Used By	Complexity	Cost
978	Excel/Google Sheets	Small offices	Very low	Free
979	POS + Inventory software	Cafes/restaurants	Medium	₹500 to ₹6000/month
980	ERP systems	Large companies	High	₹50k to lakhs
981	Custom software	Organizations with special workflows	Variable	Depends
982	
983	⸻
984	
985	Best Practical Options For Office Cafeteria
986	
987	1.  Zoho Inventory￼
988	
989	Very good for:
990	
991	1. Stock management
992	2. Purchase tracking
993	3. Vendor management
994	4. Barcode support
995	5. Reports
996	
997	Pricing starts around:
998	₹2000 to ₹5000/month for SMEs.  ￼
999	
1000	Good if:
1001	
1002	1. Your cafeteria already has structured procurement
1003	2. Multiple vendors
1004	3. Need professional reporting
1005	
1006	⸻
1007	
1008	2.  Petpooja￼
1009	
1010	Very common in Indian restaurants and cafeterias.
1011	
1012	Features:
1013	
1014	1. Billing
1015	2. Inventory
1016	3. Recipe management
1017	4. Kitchen orders
1018	5. Consumption deduction
1019	6. GST billing
1020	
1021	Pricing:
1022	Typically ₹1500 to ₹6000/month depending on features.  ￼
1023	
1024	Very suitable for:
1025	
1026	1. Cafeterias
1027	2. Food counters
1028	3. Internal food operations
1029	
1030	⸻
1031	
1032	3.  POSist by Restroworks￼
1033	
1034	More enterprise oriented.
1035	
1036	Used by:
1037	
1038	1. Restaurant chains
1039	2. Corporate cafeterias
1040	3. Multi outlet operations
1041	
1042	Features:
1043	
1044	1. Inventory
1045	2. Recipe level stock deduction
1046	3. Analytics
1047	4. Wastage tracking
1048	5. Vendor management
1049	
1050	Cost:
1051	Usually ₹4000 to ₹15000/month+ depending on scale.  ￼
1052	
1053	⸻
1054	
1055	4.  LimeTray Inventory Management￼
1056	
1057	Good for:
1058	
1059	1. Food inventory
1060	2. Raw material tracking
1061	3. Alerts
1062	4. Food wastage optimization
1063	
1064	They claim restaurants can reduce wastage significantly.  ￼
1065	
1066	⸻
1067	
1068	5.  GOFRUGAL POS￼
1069	
1070	Popular in India.
1071	
1072	Good balance between:
1073	
1074	1. POS
1075	2. Inventory
1076	3. Kitchen management
1077	4. Billing
1078	
1079	Often used in:
1080	
1081	1. Retail stores
1082	2. Cafes
1083	3. Small restaurants
1084	
1085	⸻
1086	
1087	What Hardware Would You Need?
1088	
1089	For a cafeteria:
1090	
1091	Minimal Setup
1092	
1093	Hardware	Approx Cost
1094	Barcode scanner	₹1500 to ₹4000
1095	Thermal bill printer	₹4000 to ₹10000
1096	Android tablet/PC	Existing device works
1097	Barcode labels	Optional
1098	POS terminal	Optional
1099	
1100	⸻
1101	
1102	Realistically, What Should YOU Use?
1103	
1104	Based on your profile and engineering mindset, here are the smartest paths:
1105	
1106	Option A: Ultra Lean
1107	
1108	Use:
1109	
1110	1. Google Sheets
1111	2. QR/barcode scanning app
1112	3. Simple Node.js dashboard
1113	
1114	Cost:
1115	Almost free.
1116	
1117	Good if:
1118	
1119	1. Cafeteria is small
1120	2. You want learning + experimentation
1121	3. Internal use only
1122	
1123	You could literally build this yourself.
1124	
1125	And honestly, for many office cafeterias:
1126	A custom lightweight system is often better than bloated ERP software.
1127	
1128	⸻
1129	
1130	Option B: Practical Professional Setup
1131	
1132	Use:
1133	
1134	1. Petpooja
1135	2. Zoho Inventory
1136	3. Barcode scanner
1137

1139	₹2000 to ₹8000/month total.
1140	
1141	This is probably the sweet spot.

1144	
1145	Option C: Enterprise Grade
1146	
1147	Use:
1148	
1149	1. POSist
1150	2. Oracle Simphony
1151	3. ERP integrations

1154	₹50k to lakhs/year.  ￼
1155	
1156	Overkill for most office cafeterias.
1157	
1158	⸻
1159	
1160	Important Insight
1161	
1162	Inventory systems for cafeterias are MUCH more complicated than grocery stores because of recipe consumption.
1163	
1164	Example:
1165	
1166	Selling:
1167	1 Veg sandwich
1168	
1169	Must automatically deduct:
1170	
1171	1. Bread slices
1172	2. Butter
1173	3. Tomato
1174	4. Onion
1175	5. Cheese
1176	6. Sauce
1177	
1178	This is called:
1179	Recipe mapping or BOM
1180	Bill of Materials.
1181	
1182	That is where restaurant inventory systems become powerful.
1183	
1184	⸻
1185

1187	
1188	Considering your technical skills:
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/appointy/work/OfficeOperationsUmbrella/field-app/src/utils/device.ts",
  "content": "import AsyncStorage from '@react-native-async-storage/async-storage';\nimport { nanoid } from 'nanoid/non-secure';\n\nconst KEY = 'field-app:device-id';\n\nlet cached: string | null = null;\n\nexport async function getDeviceId(): Promise<string> {\n  if (cached) return cached;\n  const stored = await AsyncStorage.getItem(KEY);\n  if (stored) {\n    cached = stored;\n    return stored;\n  }\n  const id = `dev_${nanoid(12)}`;\n  await AsyncStorage.setItem(KEY, id);\n  cached = id;\n  return id;\n}\n"
}
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/appointy/work/OfficeOperationsUmbrella/field-app/src/db/database.ts",
  "content": "import * as SQLite from 'expo-sqlite';\n\nlet dbInstance: SQLite.SQLiteDatabase | null = null;\n\nexport async function getDb(): Promise<SQLite.SQLiteDatabase> {\n  if (dbInstance) return dbInstance;\n  const db = await SQLite.openDatabaseAsync('fieldapp.db');\n  await db.execAsync(`\n    PRAGMA journal_mode = WAL;\n    PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;\n\n    CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS products (\n      barcode TEXT PRIMARY KEY,\n      name TEXT NOT NULL,\n      category TEXT,\n      unit TEXT,\n      updated_at INTEGER NOT NULL\n    );\n\n    CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS orders (\n      id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,\n      vendor TEXT,\n      expected_at INTEGER,\n      status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'open',\n      updated_at INTEGER NOT NULL\n    );\n\n    CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS order_items (\n      id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,\n      order_id TEXT NOT NULL,\n      barcode TEXT,\n      product_name TEXT NOT NULL,\n      expected_qty REAL NOT NULL,\n      received_qty REAL NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,\n      FOREIGN KEY (order_id) REFERENCES orders(id) ON DELETE CASCADE\n    );\n\n    CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS receipts (\n      id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,\n      order_id TEXT,\n      barcode TEXT NOT NULL,\n      product_name TEXT NOT NULL,\n      qty REAL NOT NULL,\n      scanned_at INTEGER NOT NULL,\n      note TEXT\n    );\n\n    CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS outbox (\n      id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,\n      kind TEXT NOT NULL,\n      payload TEXT NOT NULL,\n      status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'queued',\n      attempts INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,\n      last_error TEXT,\n      created_at INTEGER NOT NULL,\n      updated_at INTEGER NOT NULL\n    );\n\n    CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS idx_outbox_status ON outbox(status);\n    CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS idx_order_items_order ON order_items(order_id);\n  `);\n  dbInstance = db;\n  return db;\n}\n\nexport function now(): number {\n  return Date.now();\n}\n"
}
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/appointy/work/OfficeOperationsUmbrella/field-app/src/db/products.ts",
  "content": "import { getDb, now } from './database';\n\nexport type Product = {\n  barcode: string;\n  name: string;\n  category: string | null;\n  unit: string | null;\n  updated_at: number;\n};\n\nexport async function findProduct(barcode: string): Promise<Product | null> {\n  const db = await getDb();\n  const row = await db.getFirstAsync<Product>(\n    'SELECT * FROM products WHERE barcode = ?',\n    [barcode]\n  );\n  return row ?? null;\n}\n\nexport async function upsertProduct(p: Omit<Product, 'updated_at'>): Promise<Product> {\n  const db = await getDb();\n  const ts = now();\n  await db.runAsync(\n    `INSERT INTO products (barcode, name, category, unit, updated_at)\n     VALUES (?, ?, ?, ?, ?)\n     ON CONFLICT(barcode) DO UPDATE SET\n       name=excluded.name, category=excluded.category, unit=excluded.unit, updated_at=excluded.updated_at`,\n    [p.barcode, p.name, p.category, p.unit, ts]\n  );\n  return { ...p, updated_at: ts };\n}\n\nexport async function listProducts(limit = 200): Promise<Product[]> {\n  const db = await getDb();\n  return db.getAllAsync<Product>(\n    'SELECT * FROM products ORDER BY updated_at DESC LIMIT ?',\n    [limit]\n  );\n}\n"
}
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/appointy/work/OfficeOperationsUmbrella/field-app/src/db/orders.ts",
  "content": "import { getDb } from './database';\n\nexport type Order = {\n  id: string;\n  vendor: string | null;\n  expected_at: number | null;\n  status: string;\n  updated_at: number;\n};\n\nexport type OrderItem = {\n  id: string;\n  order_id: string;\n  barcode: string | null;\n  product_name: string;\n  expected_qty: number;\n  received_qty: number;\n};\n\nexport async function listOpenOrders(): Promise<Order[]> {\n  const db = await getDb();\n  return db.getAllAsync<Order>(\n    `SELECT * FROM orders WHERE status = 'open' ORDER BY expected_at ASC NULLS LAST`\n  );\n}\n\nexport async function getOrderItems(orderId: string): Promise<OrderItem[]> {\n  const db = await getDb();\n  return db.getAllAsync<OrderItem>(\n    'SELECT * FROM order_items WHERE order_id = ?',\n    [orderId]\n  );\n}\n\nexport async function findOpenItemByBarcode(barcode: string): Promise<OrderItem | null> {\n  const db = await getDb();\n  return (\n    (await db.getFirstAsync<OrderItem>(\n      `SELECT oi.* FROM order_items oi\n       JOIN orders o ON o.id = oi.order_id\n       WHERE oi.barcode = ? AND o.status = 'open'\n       ORDER BY o.expected_at ASC LIMIT 1`,\n      [barcode]\n    )) ?? null\n  );\n}\n\nexport async function addReceivedQty(itemId: string, delta: number): Promise<void> {\n  const db = await getDb();\n  await db.runAsync(\n    'UPDATE order_items SET received_qty = received_qty + ? WHERE id = ?',\n    [delta, itemId]\n  );\n}\n"
}
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/appointy/work/OfficeOperationsUmbrella/field-app/src/db/outbox.ts",
  "content": "import { nanoid } from 'nanoid/non-secure';\nimport { getDb, now } from './database';\n\nexport type OutboxKind = 'receipt' | 'product_registration';\n\nexport type OutboxRow = {\n  id: string;\n  kind: OutboxKind;\n  payload: string;\n  status: 'queued' | 'sending' | 'sent' | 'failed';\n  attempts: number;\n  last_error: string | null;\n  created_at: number;\n  updated_at: number;\n};\n\nexport async function enqueue(kind: OutboxKind, payload: object): Promise<string> {\n  const db = await getDb();\n  const id = `out_${nanoid(12)}`;\n  const ts = now();\n  await db.runAsync(\n    `INSERT INTO outbox (id, kind, payload, status, attempts, created_at, updated_at)\n     VALUES (?, ?, ?, 'queued', 0, ?, ?)`,\n    [id, kind, JSON.stringify(payload), ts, ts]\n  );\n  return id;\n}\n\nexport async function pendingCount(): Promise<number> {\n  const db = await getDb();\n  const row = await db.getFirstAsync<{ c: number }>(\n    `SELECT COUNT(*) as c FROM outbox WHERE status IN ('queued', 'failed')`\n  );\n  return row?.c ?? 0;\n}\n\nexport async function nextBatch(limit: number): Promise<OutboxRow[]> {\n  const db = await getDb();\n  return db.getAllAsync<OutboxRow>(\n    `SELECT * FROM outbox WHERE status IN ('queued', 'failed')\n     ORDER BY created_at ASC LIMIT ?`,\n    [limit]\n  );\n}\n\nexport async function markSending(id: string): Promise<void> {\n  const db = await getDb();\n  await db.runAsync(\n    `UPDATE outbox SET status = 'sending', updated_at = ? WHERE id = ?`,\n    [now(), id]\n  );\n}\n\nexport async function markSent(id: string): Promise<void> {\n  const db = await getDb();\n  await db.runAsync(\n    `UPDATE outbox SET status = 'sent', updated_at = ? WHERE id = ?`,\n    [now(), id]\n  );\n}\n\nexport async function markFailed(id: string, err: string): Promise<void> {\n  const db = await getDb();\n  await db.runAsync(\n    `UPDATE outbox SET status = 'failed', attempts = attempts + 1, last_error = ?, updated_at = ? WHERE id = ?`,\n    [err.slice(0, 500), now(), id]\n  );\n}\n"
}
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/appointy/work/OfficeOperationsUmbrella/field-app/src/sync/api.ts",
  "content": "import { config } from '../config';\nimport { getDeviceId } from '../utils/device';\n\nasync function request<T>(path: string, init?: RequestInit): Promise<T> {\n  const deviceId = await getDeviceId();\n  const res = await fetch(`${config.apiBaseUrl}${path}`, {\n    ...init,\n    headers: {\n      'Content-Type': 'application/json',\n      'X-Device-Id': deviceId,\n      'X-Site-Id': config.siteId,\n      ...(init?.headers ?? {}),\n    },\n  });\n  if (!res.ok) {\n    const text = await res.text().catch(() => '');\n    throw new Error(`HTTP ${res.status}: ${text.slice(0, 200)}`);\n  }\n  return res.json() as Promise<T>;\n}\n\nexport const api = {\n  postReceipt: (payload: object) =>\n    request<{ id: string }>(`/v1/capture/receipt`, {\n      method: 'POST',\n      body: JSON.stringify(payload),\n    }),\n\n  postProduct: (payload: object) =>\n    request<{ barcode: string }>(`/v1/capture/product`, {\n      method: 'POST',\n      body: JSON.stringify(payload),\n    }),\n};\n"
}
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/appointy/work/OfficeOperationsUmbrella/field-app/src/sync/syncService.ts",
  "content": "import * as Network from 'expo-network';\nimport { config } from '../config';\nimport { markFailed, markSending, markSent, nextBatch, pendingCount } from '../db/outbox';\nimport { api } from './api';\n\ntype Listener = (count: number) => void;\n\nlet timer: ReturnType<typeof setInterval> | null = null;\nlet running = false;\nconst listeners = new Set<Listener>();\n\nexport function onPendingChange(l: Listener): () => void {\n  listeners.add(l);\n  pendingCount().then(l).catch(() => {});\n  return () => listeners.delete(l);\n}\n\nasync function notify() {\n  const c = await pendingCount().catch(() => 0);\n  listeners.forEach((l) => l(c));\n}\n\nexport async function flushOnce(): Promise<{ sent: number; failed: number }> {\n  if (running) return { sent: 0, failed: 0 };\n  running = true;\n  let sent = 0;\n  let failed = 0;\n  try {\n    const net = await Network.getNetworkStateAsync().catch(() => ({ isConnected: false }));\n    if (!net.isConnected) return { sent, failed };\n\n    const batch = await nextBatch(config.outboxBatchSize);\n    for (const row of batch) {\n      try {\n        await markSending(row.id);\n        const payload = JSON.parse(row.payload);\n        if (row.kind === 'receipt') await api.postReceipt(payload);\n        else if (row.kind === 'product_registration') await api.postProduct(payload);\n        await markSent(row.id);\n        sent++;\n      } catch (err) {\n        await markFailed(row.id, err instanceof Error ? err.message : String(err));\n        failed++;\n      }\n    }\n  } finally {\n    running = false;\n    await notify();\n  }\n  return { sent, failed };\n}\n\nexport function startSync(): void {\n  if (timer) return;\n  flushOnce().catch(() => {});\n  timer = setInterval(() => {\n    flushOnce().catch(() => {});\n  }, config.syncIntervalMs);\n}\n\nexport function stopSync(): void {\n  if (timer) clearInterval(timer);\n  timer = null;\n}\n"
}
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/appointy/work/OfficeOperationsUmbrella/field-app/src/components/BigButton.tsx",
  "content": "import React from 'react';\nimport { Pressable, StyleSheet, Text, View, ViewStyle } from 'react-native';\nimport { colors } from '../theme/colors';\nimport { fontSize, radius, spacing } from '../theme/spacing';\nimport { haptic } from '../utils/haptics';\n\ntype Props = {\n  label: string;\n  icon?: string;\n  onPress: () => void;\n  variant?: 'primary' | 'success' | 'danger' | 'muted';\n  style?: ViewStyle;\n  disabled?: boolean;\n};\n\nconst variantBg: Record<NonNullable<Props['variant']>, string> = {\n  primary: colors.primary,\n  success: colors.success,\n  danger: colors.danger,\n  muted: colors.bgMuted,\n};\n\nexport function BigButton({ label, icon, onPress, variant = 'primary', style, disabled }: Props) {\n  const isMuted = variant === 'muted';\n  return (\n    <Pressable\n      onPress={() => {\n        haptic.tap();\n        onPress();\n      }}\n      disabled={disabled}\n      style={({ pressed }) => [\n        styles.btn,\n        { backgroundColor: variantBg[variant], opacity: disabled ? 0.5 : pressed ? 0.85 : 1 },\n        style,\n      ]}\n    >\n      <View style={styles.row}>\n        {icon ? <Text style={[styles.icon, isMuted && { color: colors.text }]}>{icon}</Text> : null}\n        <Text style={[styles.label, isMuted && { color: colors.text }]}>{label}</Text>\n      </View>\n    </Pressable>\n  );\n}\n\nconst styles = StyleSheet.create({\n  btn: {\n    minHeight: 72,\n    borderRadius: radius.lg,\n    paddingHorizontal: spacing.lg,\n    paddingVertical: spacing.md,\n    justifyContent: 'center',\n  },\n  row: { flexDirection: 'row', alignItems: 'center', justifyContent: 'center' },\n  icon: { fontSize: fontSize.xl, color: '#fff', marginRight: spacing.md },\n  label: { fontSize: fontSize.lg, color: '#fff', fontWeight: '700' },\n});\n"
}
```

```json
  "file_path": "/Users/appointy/work/OfficeOperationsUmbrella/field-app/src/components/QueueBadge.tsx",
  "content": "import React, { useEffect, useState } from 'react';\nimport { StyleSheet, Text, View } from 'react-native';\nimport { onPendingChange } from '../sync/syncService';\nimport { colors } from '../theme/colors';\nimport { fontSize, radius, spacing } from '../theme/spacing';\n\nexport function QueueBadge() {\n  const [count, setCount] = useState(0);\n  useEffect(() => onPendingChange(setCount), []);\n  const ok = count === 0;\n  return (\n    <View style={[styles.box, { backgroundColor: ok ? colors.success : colors.warn }]}>\n      <Text style={styles.text}>{ok ? '✓ All synced' : `${count} waiting to send`}</Text>\n    </View>\n  );\n}\n\nconst styles = StyleSheet.create({\n  box: {\n    paddingVertical: spacing.sm,\n    paddingHorizontal: spacing.md,\n    borderRadius: radius.md,\n    alignSelf: 'flex-start',\n  },\n  text: { color: '#fff', fontWeight: '700', fontSize: fontSize.md },\n});\n"
}
```

```json
  "content": "import { NativeStackScreenProps } from '@react-navigation/native-stack';\nimport React from 'react';\nimport { SafeAreaView, StyleSheet, Text, View } from 'react-native';\nimport { BigButton } from '../components/BigButton';\nimport { QueueBadge } from '../components/QueueBadge';\nimport { RootStackParamList } from '../navigation/types';\nimport { colors } from '../theme/colors';\nimport { fontSize, spacing } from '../theme/spacing';\n\ntype Props = NativeStackScreenProps<RootStackParamList, 'Home'>;\n\nexport function HomeScreen({ navigation }: Props) {\n  return (\n    <SafeAreaView style={styles.safe}>\n      <View style={styles.header}>\n        <Text style={styles.title}>Field App</Text>\n        <QueueBadge />\n      </View>\n\n      <View style={styles.actions}>\n        <BigButton\n          label=\"Scan Item\"\n          icon=\"📷\"\n          onPress={() => navigation.navigate('Scanner')}\n          style={styles.bigCta}\n        />\n        <BigButton\n          label=\"Expected Deliveries\"\n          icon=\"📋\"\n          variant=\"muted\"\n          onPress={() => navigation.navigate('Orders')}\n        />\n        <BigButton\n          label=\"Sync Queue\"\n          icon=\"🔄\"\n          variant=\"muted\"\n          onPress={() => navigation.navigate('Outbox')}\n        />\n      </View>\n    </SafeAreaView>\n  );\n}\n\nconst styles = StyleSheet.create({\n  safe: { flex: 1, backgroundColor: colors.bg, padding: spacing.lg },\n  header: { marginBottom: spacing.xl },\n  title: { fontSize: fontSize.xxl, fontWeight: '800', color: colors.text, marginBottom: spacing.md },\n  actions: { gap: spacing.md },\n  bigCta: { minHeight: 140 },\n});\n"
}
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/appointy/work/OfficeOperationsUmbrella/field-app/src/screens/ScannerScreen.tsx",
  "content": "import { CameraView, useCameraPermissions } from 'expo-camera';\nimport { NativeStackScreenProps } from '@react-navigation/native-stack';\nimport React, { useCallback, useEffect, useRef, useState } from 'react';\nimport { SafeAreaView, StyleSheet, Text, View } from 'react-native';\nimport { BigButton } from '../components/BigButton';\nimport { findProduct } from '../db/products';\nimport { RootStackParamList } from '../navigation/types';\nimport { colors } from '../theme/colors';\nimport { fontSize, spacing } from '../theme/spacing';\nimport { haptic } from '../utils/haptics';\n\ntype Props = NativeStackScreenProps<RootStackParamList, 'Scanner'>;\n\nconst SUPPORTED = ['ean13', 'ean8', 'upc_a', 'upc_e', 'code128', 'code39', 'qr'] as const;\n\nexport function ScannerScreen({ navigation }: Props) {\n  const [permission, requestPermission] = useCameraPermissions();\n  const [lastCode, setLastCode] = useState<string | null>(null);\n  const lockedRef = useRef(false);\n\n  useEffect(() => {\n    if (permission && !permission.granted && permission.canAskAgain) requestPermission();\n  }, [permission, requestPermission]);\n\n  const handleScan = useCallback(\n    async ({ data }: { data: string }) => {\n      if (lockedRef.current || !data) return;\n      lockedRef.current = true;\n      setLastCode(data);\n      haptic.success();\n      const existing = await findProduct(data).catch(() => null);\n      if (existing) navigation.replace('Receiving', { barcode: data });\n      else navigation.replace('RegisterProduct', { barcode: data });\n    },\n    [navigation]\n  );\n\n  if (!permission) {\n    return (\n      <SafeAreaView style={styles.center}>\n        <Text style={styles.info}>Loading camera…</Text>\n      </SafeAreaView>\n    );\n  }\n\n  if (!permission.granted) {\n    return (\n      <SafeAreaView style={styles.center}>\n        <Text style={styles.info}>Camera permission is required to scan items.</Text>\n        <BigButton label=\"Allow camera\" icon=\"📷\" onPress={requestPermission} />\n      </SafeAreaView>\n    );\n  }\n\n  return (\n    <SafeAreaView style={styles.safe}>\n      <View style={styles.cameraWrap}>\n        <CameraView\n          style={StyleSheet.absoluteFill}\n          facing=\"back\"\n          barcodeScannerSettings={{ barcodeTypes: [...SUPPORTED] }}\n          onBarcodeScanned={handleScan}\n        />\n        <View style={styles.frame} pointerEvents=\"none\" />\n      </View>\n\n      <View style={styles.footer}>\n        <Text style={styles.hint}>{lastCode ? `Scanned: ${lastCode}` : 'Point camera at barcode'}</Text>\n        <BigButton label=\"Cancel\" variant=\"muted\" onPress={() => navigation.goBack()} />\n      </View>\n    </SafeAreaView>\n  );\n}\n\nconst styles = StyleSheet.create({\n  safe: { flex: 1, backgroundColor: colors.text },\n  center: { flex: 1, backgroundColor: colors.bg, padding: spacing.lg, justifyContent: 'center', gap: spacing.md },\n  info: { fontSize: fontSize.md, color: colors.text, textAlign: 'center', marginBottom: spacing.md },\n  cameraWrap: { flex: 1, overflow: 'hidden' },\n  frame: {\n    position: 'absolute',\n    top: '25%',\n    left: '10%',\n    right: '10%',\n    bottom: '35%',\n    borderWidth: 3,\n    borderColor: '#fff',\n    borderRadius: 16,\n  },\n  footer: { padding: spacing.lg, backgroundColor: colors.text, gap: spacing.md },\n  hint: { color: '#fff', fontSize: fontSize.md, textAlign: 'center' },\n});\n"
}
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/appointy/work/OfficeOperationsUmbrella/field-app/src/screens/RegisterProductScreen.tsx",
  "content": "import { NativeStackScreenProps } from '@react-navigation/native-stack';\nimport React, { useState } from 'react';\nimport { Pressable, SafeAreaView, ScrollView, StyleSheet, Text, TextInput, View } from 'react-native';\nimport { BigButton } from '../components/BigButton';\nimport { enqueue } from '../db/outbox';\nimport { upsertProduct } from '../db/products';\nimport { RootStackParamList } from '../navigation/types';\nimport { flushOnce } from '../sync/syncService';\nimport { colors } from '../theme/colors';\nimport { fontSize, radius, spacing } from '../theme/spacing';\nimport { haptic } from '../utils/haptics';\n\ntype Props = NativeStackScreenProps<RootStackParamList, 'RegisterProduct'>;\n\nconst CATEGORIES = ['Grocery', 'Cleaning', 'Office', 'Cafeteria', 'Other'];\nconst UNITS = ['Piece', 'Kg', 'Litre', 'Pack', 'Box'];\n\nexport function RegisterProductScreen({ route, navigation }: Props) {\n  const { barcode } = route.params;\n  const [name, setName] = useState('');\n  const [category, setCategory] = useState<string>('Grocery');\n  const [unit, setUnit] = useState<string>('Piece');\n  const [saving, setSaving] = useState(false);\n\n  const onSave = async () => {\n    if (!name.trim()) {\n      haptic.warn();\n      return;\n    }\n    setSaving(true);\n    try {\n      await upsertProduct({ barcode, name: name.trim(), category, unit });\n      await enqueue('product_registration', { barcode, name: name.trim(), category, unit });\n      flushOnce().catch(() => {});\n      haptic.success();\n      navigation.replace('Receiving', { barcode });\n    } finally {\n      setSaving(false);\n    }\n  };\n\n  return (\n    <SafeAreaView style={styles.safe}>\n      <ScrollView contentContainerStyle={styles.scroll}>\n        <Text style={styles.h1}>New product</Text>\n        <Text style={styles.meta}>Barcode: {barcode}</Text>\n\n        <Text style={styles.label}>Name</Text>\n        <TextInput\n          value={name}\n          onChangeText={setName}\n          placeholder=\"e.g. Tata Salt 1kg\"\n          style={styles.input}\n          autoFocus\n        />\n\n        <Text style={styles.label}>Category</Text>\n        <View style={styles.chipRow}>\n          {CATEGORIES.map((c) => (\n            <Chip key={c} label={c} active={c === category} onPress={() => setCategory(c)} />\n          ))}\n        </View>\n\n        <Text style={styles.label}>Unit</Text>\n        <View style={styles.chipRow}>\n          {UNITS.map((u) => (\n            <Chip key={u} label={u} active={u === unit} onPress={() => setUnit(u)} />\n          ))}\n        </View>\n\n        <View style={{ height: spacing.lg }} />\n        <BigButton\n          label={saving ? 'Saving…' : 'Save & continue'}\n          icon=\"✓\"\n          variant=\"success\"\n          onPress={onSave}\n          disabled={saving || !name.trim()}\n        />\n        <BigButton label=\"Cancel\" variant=\"muted\" onPress={() => navigation.popToTop()} />\n      </ScrollView>\n    </SafeAreaView>\n  );\n}\n\nfunction Chip({ label, active, onPress }: { label: string; active: boolean; onPress: () => void }) {\n  return (\n    <Pressable\n      onPress={() => {\n        haptic.tap();\n        onPress();\n      }}\n      style={[styles.chip, active && styles.chipActive]}\n    >\n      <Text style={[styles.chipText, active && styles.chipTextActive]}>{label}</Text>\n    </Pressable>\n  );\n}\n\nconst styles = StyleSheet.create({\n  safe: { flex: 1, backgroundColor: colors.bg },\n  scroll: { padding: spacing.lg, gap: spacing.sm },\n  h1: { fontSize: fontSize.xl, fontWeight: '800', color: colors.text },\n  meta: { fontSize: fontSize.md, color: colors.textMuted, marginBottom: spacing.md },\n  label: { fontSize: fontSize.md, fontWeight: '700', color: colors.text, marginTop: spacing.md },\n  input: {\n    borderWidth: 2,\n    borderColor: colors.border,\n    borderRadius: radius.md,\n    padding: spacing.md,\n    fontSize: fontSize.lg,\n    backgroundColor: '#fff',\n  },\n  chipRow: { flexDirection: 'row', flexWrap: 'wrap', gap: spacing.sm },\n  chip: {\n    paddingVertical: spacing.sm,\n    paddingHorizontal: spacing.md,\n    borderRadius: radius.lg,\n    borderWidth: 2,\n    borderColor: colors.border,\n    backgroundColor: '#fff',\n  },\n  chipActive: { backgroundColor: colors.primary, borderColor: colors.primary },\n  chipText: { fontSize: fontSize.md, color: colors.text, fontWeight: '600' },\n  chipTextActive: { color: '#fff' },\n});\n"
}
```
